  • Timeline. The Committee renders its decision within 30 business days of escalation, with one 15-day extension permitted for cases requiring additional evidence or expert consultation.
  • Transparency. The Program Operator publishes anonymized Appeals Committee outcomes in the annual transparency report, including the number of appeals heard, the disposition (affirm/reverse/remand), and the categories of issue raised. Individual brand identities and SKU-specific details are not disclosed.
  • Funding. The Appeals Committee operates on a per-appeal fee structure paid by the Program Operator and a per-Licensee contribution from the licensing fee schedule. This funding arrangement ensures that the Committee is not financially dependent on case outcomes and that no individual appeal carries financial pressure for the reviewers.

Tier 2 review is binding. A Licensee that prevails at Tier 2 has the Program Operator's status determination reversed, with retroactive effect to the original determination date.

14.2 Reinstatement After Suspension or Revocation

Reinstatement requires:

  • Completion of CAPA with verified effectiveness (per Part 2.8);
  • A defined number of consecutive compliant lots (set case-by-case by the Program Operator); and
  • Restored data integrity (per Part 2.7).

The Program Operator determines specific reinstatement requirements on a case-by-case basis. The specific number of compliant lots, the depth of CAPA review, the extent of independent verification, and the timeline are calibrated to the severity and nature of the original nonconformance.

Revocation (Status E) requires full re-application to the Program at the Program Operator's discretion. A brand whose license was revoked for data integrity violations or willful misconduct faces the highest reinstatement bar; the Program Operator's discretion to refuse re-application is broad. This is consistent with Part 2.3's framing: Status E is the program's mechanism for terminating relationships that cannot be repaired through standard remediation.

14.3 Document Precedence

In the event of any discrepancy between documents within the HMTc Program (including between this Program Manual, the Governance Policy, and the Lot Testing Schedule), the Program Operator will issue a formal Technical Clarification.

Until such clarification is issued, the more conservative interpretation governs. "More conservative" means the interpretation that results in stricter limits, tighter testing requirements, or more rigorous CAPA obligations. This default ensures that ambiguity in the document set does not advantage a Licensee at the consumer's expense.

The Program Operator maintains the operative version of each controlled document under document control with version numbers, effective dates, and change logs. The version in effect at the time of a certification decision governs that decision. Subsequent revisions do not retroactively alter prior decisions.

All revisions are managed under document control procedures with transition periods for new requirements. Material changes, those that would alter eligibility, testing frequency, status criteria, or CAPA obligations, receive minimum transition periods consistent with the Standards Ratchet's 12-to-18-month rule in Part 2.10.

14.4 Conflict of Interest

Individuals involved in certification determinations shall be free from conflicts of interest with the Licensee under review. The Program Operator maintains impartiality and objectivity throughout the certification process. Appeal and special committee members shall be independent from the day-to-day business decisions of the affected Licensee.

The conflict-of-interest standard applies at the individual level (no individual reviewer with a financial interest in or personal relationship to the Licensee may participate in their certification or appeal proceedings) and at the institutional level (the Paleo Foundation discloses any institutional relationships that could create the appearance of conflict). The Standards Architect's dual role as Paleo Foundation CEO is disclosed publicly in the Colophon (Part 5.7) and addressed in the Program-Level Adversarial Read (Part 5.2) on the merits.

Closing of Part 2

Part 2 has now specified the HMTc Program in its operational form. A reader who has worked through chapters 2.0 through 2.12 can:

  • Trace a SKU's lifecycle from inquiry to ratchet review via the program-flow diagram (2.0).
  • Understand the program's foundational principles and international scope (2.1).
  • Locate any analyte in the Tier 1/Tier 2 classification and understand its enforcement consequence (2.2).
  • Read the five-status framework with its eligibility criteria, mark-usage permissions, testing frequencies, CAPA requirements, and escalation paths (2.3).
  • Understand the Confidential Remediation Track and its legal-defensibility role (2.4).
  • Apply mark-usage rules and anti-arbitrage provisions (2.5).
  • Operate the two-sided measurement-uncertainty decision rule (2.6).
  • Comply with structured-data and data-integrity requirements (2.7).
  • Initiate and close CAPAs according to status-specific timelines (2.8).
  • Receive and respond to supplier-intelligence alerts (2.9).
  • Plan against the Standards Ratchet trigger conditions, step cap, transition rules, and forward forecast (2.10).
  • Handle regulatory escalation as a hard-stop condition (2.11).
  • Appeal status determinations, recover from suspension, and operate under document control (2.12).

Part 2.5, which follows, demonstrates how brands and QA teams use heavymetalindex.com, the public corpus introduced in Part 1.5, as an active operational tool. Part 3 publishes the per-product standards.

Part 2.5, Using heavymetalindex.com

Part 1.5 introduced heavymetalindex.com as the public curated corpus of the heavy-metals-in-food literature that HMTc's standards rest on. That chapter established the architectural separation between HMI and the certification program and explained why the separation produces durable epistemic authority. This chapter is the operational complement: it demonstrates how brand QA teams, brand legal teams, and certification auditors use the corpus day-to-day in the course of operating under HMTc.

The chapter has four sections. The first three describe how the corpus serves brand operations today; the fourth describes a roadmap capability, the brand-facing percentile-placement dashboard, that is on the program's near-term build path and that brands considering certification should expect to come online during their first license cycle.

This chapter exists for a strategic reason. The certification mark is the program's visible signal; the corpus is the program's value engine. A reader who finishes Part 2.5 should understand that HMTc is not just a certification mark backed by a methodology, but a certification mark backed by a continuously-maintained operational tool that brand QA teams lean on. That distinction is what separates HMTc from any single-point certification.

2.5.1 The diagnostic tool

When a brand's lot tests come back with one or more analytes elevated above their typical range, even when still within the HMTc limit, the brand's first operational question is: why? Random analytical noise, a seasonal supplier shift, a new ingredient lot, a process change that wasn't documented as material, an upstream supply-chain event the brand has not yet been alerted to? The diagnostic tool is the relevant ingredient pages on heavymetalindex.com.

Each ingredient page on HMI carries a structured contamination_profile block summarizing what the literature says about typical and 95th-percentile concentrations for each priority metal in that commodity, with three additional structured layers:

  • Geographic variance, typical concentration ranges by growing region, with sample sizes from contributing sources. A brand sourcing cocoa from Ecuador can see what the published literature reports for Cd in Ecuadorian cocoa specifically, distinguished from Cd in cocoa from Côte d'Ivoire, Ghana, or Indonesia.
  • Seasonal variance, concentration ranges by harvest period and climate condition, where the literature reports them. The El Niño years 2015–2016 and 2023 are documented as drought-stress periods that elevate Cd uptake in Ecuadorian cocoa by roughly 40% above non-El-Niño baselines.
  • Processing effects, how polishing, rinsing, parboiling, fermentation, drying, milling, and refining shift concentrations of which metals. Each processing-effect claim is anchored to a cited source.

Worked example

A brand producing chocolate-flavored toddler snack pouches sees their July 2026 lot test return Cd at 240 ppb against an HMTc limit of 350 ppb. The result is within limit, but it is meaningfully higher than the brand's typical 90 ppb baseline. Brand QA opens heavymetalindex.com/ingredients/cocoa, navigates to the Cd contamination_profile block, and finds:

  • Typical Cd range across all reported cocoa: 80–240 ppb (p95: 410 ppb).
  • Geographic breakdown shows Ecuadorian high-altitude cocoa at 220–540 ppb (n=87 from one peer-reviewed source) versus Ghanaian cocoa at 70–210 ppb (n=92 from a separate source).
  • Seasonal variance shows Ecuadorian cocoa during El Niño years at 180–620 ppb (n=41), running roughly 40% above non-El-Niño baselines.

The brand cross-references their supplier records: their June 2026 cocoa lot was sourced from a new Ecuadorian high-altitude supplier (replacing their typical Ghanaian supplier due to a price shift), and the 2025-2026 El Niño cycle was active during the relevant harvest period. The diagnostic answer is clear, traceable to the literature, and actionable: source verification on the new supplier, possibly a return to the Ghanaian supplier or a regional diversification of cocoa sourcing.

This diagnostic conversation happened in twenty minutes using the corpus. It would have taken brand QA days of supplier outreach and ingredient testing to reach the same conclusion working from scratch.

2.5.2 The remediation playbook

Each ingredient page on HMI carries a Mitigation Options section ranked by impact magnitude. The categories are: sourcing levers (origin region, supplier specification, soil-Cd or paddy-iAs pre-screening); agronomic levers (soil amendments, water management, cultivar selection); processing levers (rinsing, polishing, fermentation, drying); formulation levers (substitute or dilute); testing and QC levers (lot-level vs. spot-check, internal vs. third-party); and packaging and storage levers where relevant.

Each lever entry names the magnitude of the effect on the metal in question and cites the source page that establishes the effect. A brand reading the cocoa ingredient page's Mitigation Options finds, for Cd:

  • Sourcing level (highest impact): regional substitution from Ecuadorian high-altitude to Ghanaian or West African low-altitude supply can reduce typical Cd by 60-70% (anchored to comparative studies cited on the page).
  • Agronomic level: soil-Cd pre-screening of farmer plots before harvest contracting can reduce supplier-batch variance by 40-50%.
  • Processing level: fermentation duration and pH adjustments during cocoa processing can shift Cd uptake by 15-25%.
  • Formulation level: dilution with lower-Cd ingredients reduces final-product Cd proportionally (a 50/50 blend of high-Cd and low-Cd cocoa typically produces ~50% of the high-Cd value, allowing for ingredient interactions documented in the cited sources).
  • Testing and QC level: lot-by-lot incoming-cocoa testing with rejection thresholds at the 80% PoL internal warning level can prevent elevated lots from entering production.

Brand QA can read the cited sources for each lever, evaluate which fit their operational reality (sourcing changes have long lead times; processing changes have capital implications; formulation changes have R&D and labeling implications), and assemble a defensible reformulation plan. The remediation pathway becomes a reading exercise, not a research project.

For brands enrolled in the Confidential Remediation Track, this is the daily operational tool the Track's technical guidance points them to. The Track's value is not opacity but accelerated access to the same evidence base that successful Status A brands have already used to reduce their contamination profiles.

2.5.3 Percentile placement (roadmap)

A future capability on the HMTc roadmap is the brand-facing percentile-placement dashboard. The dashboard will allow a brand with a license to log into the heavymetaltested.com platform and see, in real time:

  • Where their lot results sit relative to the pooled distribution of all certified Status A SKUs for the same product category and metal. A brand's lot at 12 ppb Pb in a category with a 30 ppb HMTc limit and a Status A median of 8 ppb sees themselves at "P67 of certified Status A pool", better than two-thirds of certified competitors, worse than one-third.
  • Their trend over time, with the pool's distribution overlay. Is the brand improving faster than the certified market, slower, or at the same pace? Is the brand's percentile placement getting better or worse as the certified pool tightens around them?
  • The projected next-cycle limit (per Part 2.10's forward forecast) overlaid on their current lot trajectory. Will the brand's current trajectory clear the projected next-cycle limit before enforcement, or will the brand need to accelerate reduction?

This is the operational expression of the Standards Ratchet's planning-tool framing. A brand that knows where they sit and where they are heading can budget reformulation against actual data rather than abstract limits. A brand that is at the cleaner end of the certified pool sees their leadership reflected in real time; a brand at the dirtier end of the pool sees the urgency of their reformulation runway.

The dashboard is a roadmap capability rather than a current operational feature. Brands considering certification should expect the dashboard to come online during their first license cycle; brands currently certified will receive access as the capability ships. The roadmap is named here so that brands evaluating HMTc understand the operational trajectory they are signing up for, not just the standards they are signing up against.

2.5.4 The pre-certification baseline

Before a brand signs an HMTc license agreement, the brand can use heavymetalindex.com to model their pre-certification baseline. The exercise is straightforward and takes one to two hours per product category.

The brand identifies the product-category page on HMI that matches their SKU (one of the fifteen Category 1 pages, or a Category 2+ page when those come online). The product-category page carries a Master Limit Table previewing what the HMTc limit would be for each priority analyte, the applicable regulatory cap, and the basis note. The brand cross-references this against their internal testing data, if any, or against the relevant ingredient pages' contamination_profile blocks if no internal data exists.

This pre-certification exercise tells the brand:

  • Whether their current production is likely to qualify for Status A immediately (most metals at or below the HMTc limit), Status B (one or more Tier 2 metals between 100% and 150% PoL), or only the Confidential Remediation Track.
  • Which metals are closest to the limit, the analytes most likely to drive a status determination.
  • Which ingredients in their formulation are the likely contributors, sourcing lever candidates.
  • What the rough magnitude of reformulation effort would be, small adjustments versus full sourcing-chain restructuring.

A brand whose pre-certification baseline suggests Status A or B can sign the license with reasonable confidence about their initial status. A brand whose pre-certification baseline suggests Status C-or-worse can sign the license with the understanding that the Confidential Remediation Track is their entry path, and can begin the technical-guidance conversations with the Program Operator before formal enrollment. Either way, the brand enters the program with their eyes open and their reformulation strategy informed by the same evidence base the Program Operator is using to set their limits.

This is the closing argument for the chapter and for the corpus's role in the Program. The HMTc certification mark is the visible signal. The HMI corpus is the operational tool that brand QA, brand legal, and brand management use to plan, diagnose, remediate, and verify the certification effort. The two work together; neither alone would be sufficient. Part 3 publishes the standards that the corpus supports; Part 4 specifies the testing schedule that operationalizes the surveillance; Part 5 documents the methodology that the corpus feeds. The bound volume in front of the reader is the program's specification; heavymetalindex.com is where the program lives.

Cross-references

  • The corpus's structure and the wiki/HMTc firewall: Part 1.5.
  • The product-category pages corresponding to Part 3's per-product chapters: wiki/products/* on heavymetalindex.com.
  • The ingredient pages with contamination profiles, geographic variance, seasonal variance, and Mitigation Options: wiki/ingredients/* on heavymetalindex.com.
  • The metal pages with toxicology, exposure routes, and regulatory limits: wiki/metals/* on heavymetalindex.com (the toxicology callouts in Part 2.2 reference these directly).
  • The Standards Ratchet forward forecast: Part 2.10.
  • The Confidential Remediation Track: Part 2.4.

Part 3 — The Standards

This Part publishes the per-category HMTc standards for the fifteen product chapters covered by the 2026 Edition. Each chapter is self-contained enough to function as a licensing exhibit detached from the bound volume; when a brand licenses HMTc certification for a specific product, the relevant chapter is attached as an exhibit to the licensing agreement and stands on its own.

Structure of each chapter

Each Part 3 chapter contains:

  • Master Limit Table — the ten priority analytes (eight metals plus speciated iAs and MeHg where applicable), each with the HMTc action level, the applicable regulatory cap (FDA / EU / state, lowest applicable), and a basis note clarifying which value binds (literature-baseline / regulatory-alignment / precautionary / feasibility-driven).
  • Methodology summary — the per-analyte percentile mechanism (P97 clean / P45 dirty per the Governing Principles), the row-pass calibration intent (90% clean platform / 30% dirty platform achieving Status A), and the regulatory-cap-as-ceiling rule.
  • Per-analyte sections — for the priority metals in this category (those closest to limits or most consequential for the matrix), a focused per-analyte narrative covering: HMTc limit and rationale; cited literature evidence anchoring the limit; toxicology callout (drawn from the canonical metal page on heavymetalindex.com per Part 5.3 Glossary); regulatory cross-reference; and applicable speciation requirement.
  • Adversarial Read for this product — the categories of attack a plaintiff's expert or hostile reviewer might advance against this specific product's standards, and the program's response on the merits. (Program-level attacks that apply across all categories are addressed in Part 5.2 rather than duplicated per-chapter.)
  • Sources Cited — chapter-scoped citations resolving to source pages on heavymetalindex.com. Numbered aliases for inline use.
  • Provenance Footer — chapter-scoped: the pooled-distribution row hash, the briefing-generator commit, and the generation timestamp.

Chapter list

How the Part 3 chapters were produced

Each chapter is the deterministic output of a Standards Briefing generator (tools/standards/render-part3-chapter.mjs in the project repository) that retemplates the underlying Standards Briefing into the bound-manual format. The retemplating applies the Part 2.2 / Part 5.3 toxicology callout references, prunes engine-implementation vocabulary per the wiki/HMTc firewall, and adds chapter-level cross-references to Parts 2, 4, and 5. The substantive content (Master Limit Table values, per-analyte rationale, sources cited, provenance footer) is preserved from the underlying Standards Briefing without modification.

3.1 Infant Formula, Powder (Non-Soy)

This chapter is one of fifteen per-product chapters in Part 3 of the HMTc Program Manual. Each chapter is self-contained enough to function as a licensing exhibit detached from the bound volume. The methodology that produces the limit values in this chapter is documented in Part 5.1 (How HMTc Standards Are Set). The certification status framework that operationalizes the limits is documented in Part 2.3. The Standards Ratchet that evolves these limits over time is documented in Part 2.10. Program-level adversarial attacks not specific to this product are addressed in Part 5.2 (Program-Level Adversarial Read); the per-product adversarial read in this chapter addresses attacks specific to this product category.

Product class: Infant Formula, Powder (Non-Soy) Subcategory role: clean subcategory (within-pair) Native basis for published thresholds: powder_as_placed Methodology: HMTc-10 analyte panel; per-analyte standards derived from pooled occurrence distribution at P97 (clean / independent rows) or P45 (dirty rows), capped at the lowest applicable regulatory ceiling. See "Methodology in one page" below. Date generated: 2026-05-14 Author: Karen Pendergrass, Standards Architect, Paleo Foundation Status: Draft for stakeholder review. Not yet through the 9 pre-publication gates.

Who this briefing is for

The HMTc certification mark serves multiple audiences with different concerns. This briefing is written so each audience finds what they need in one document. Audience-tagged "read" callouts appear inline next to each per-analyte section.

Brand legal teams, Read the per-analyte Defensibility narrative and the Adversarial read section. The methodology section is your Daubert-resistance reference. Every numeric claim traces to a source page; the contribution logs document admission criteria.

Retailer quality and compliance, Read the Master Limit Table and the per-analyte Regulator read callouts. The certification mark on a product means the brand has tested at or below every value in the Master Limit Table, in the product's native basis. The regulatory comparison rows show which government caps apply and how HMTc thresholds compare.

Regulators (FDA, EFSA, state AGs), Read the Methodology section, the Sources Cited table, and the Anti-Circumvention argument. The methodology is open, reproducible, and follows the WHO/JECFA, EFSA, ATSDR, and Codex precedents cited. Numeric thresholds are derived from a pooled occurrence distribution of peer-reviewed and agency data, weighted by source N with a 50% relative-share cap.

Adversarial readers (plaintiff's experts, competing standards bodies), Read the per-analyte Defensibility narrative, the Adversarial read section, the contribution logs, and the sensitivity analyses. Every value carries: the pool composition, the basis-conversion math, the regulatory cap comparison, the parametric sensitivity, and the rationale for any gap between literature and threshold.

All ten HMTc analytes for this product class. Each row shows the published HMTc threshold (rounded up to the primary increment ladder), the precise pooled-engine value before rounding, the rationale tag, the contributing source count, the parametric-sensitivity band, and the lowest applicable regulatory ceiling. Brands meeting every value in this table on a like-for-like basis qualify for the HMTc certification mark for infant formula powder non soy.

How to read this table. "Published" values are rounded up to the HMTc primary increment ladder (1, 2, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25, 50, 100, 200, 500, 1000, 1300, 1500, 2000, 5000, 10000 ppb) per the program's publication conventions; this gives brands an unambiguous bin to test against. The "precise" column shows the pooled-engine output before rounding so the methodology is transparent and re-derivable. Rationale tags map to the four allowed rationale tags: literature-baseline (pooled value binds), regulatory-alignment (regulatory ceiling binds), feasibility-driven (analytical method binds), and precautionary (manual override below pooled and regulatory).

Methodology, one page

Per-analyte standards. For each (product row, analyte) cell, every source loaded for that cell contributes to a pooled distribution via moment-matching pseudo-sample expansion. Sample-level data uses raw values; summary statistics (mean, median, max, lb/ub, reported percentiles) reconstruct under log-normal or Tobit assumptions documented per source. Pseudo-samples are weighted by source N and capped at 50% relative pool share to prevent any one survey from dominating. The pooled distribution is sorted and queried at the per-analyte target percentile:

  • Clean subcategory: standard = pooled P97
  • Dirty subcategory: standard = pooled P45
  • Independent (no within-pair partner): standard = pooled P97 by default

Why P97 / P45, not P90 / P30 (the row-pass calibration). A brand certifies a product (one row, ten analytes simultaneously), not one analyte at a time. Setting each per-analyte limit at the raw P90 of the clean distribution would deliver a joint row-pass rate close to 0.9^10 = 35% under analyte independence. Under realistic analyte correlation (~0.6, since supply-chain hygiene drives multiple metals together), the joint rate sits around 50-70%. To deliver the row-level outcomes the Governing Principles require, approximately 90% of clean-subcategory brands and approximately 30% of dirty-subcategory brands pass row certification, per-analyte limits are uplifted to P97 (clean) and P45 (dirty). This is the calibration that delivers the program's stated objective. ALARA and the §80th-percentile ratchet trigger drive post-publication tightening; per-analyte differentiation (e.g., Pb tighter than Ni) is deferred until HMTc has 6-12 months of lot-testing data to measure actual joint pass rates.

Basis. Each product row's standard is published in the native basis of the product form: powder rows in powder-as-placed-on-market, ready-to-feed liquid rows in as-consumed, dry cereal rows in dry-as-sold, puree rows in as-consumed. Brands ship and test in their product's native form; the certification mark matches that form. Sources reporting in non-native bases are converted into the row's native basis during pooling via documented factors (the 1:7 reconstitution ratio for powder ↔ prepared; within-class identity for as_sold ↔ dry_as_sold). The cross-basis equivalent of every published value is shown in the Master Limit Table for ease of reference by audiences operating in different bases.

Regulatory cap. The final published HMTc value never exceeds the lowest applicable loaded regulatory ceiling, expressed in the product's native basis. When the pooled P97/P45 exceeds the regulatory cap, the cap binds and the rationale tag is regulatory-alignment. When the pooled value is below the cap, the rationale is literature-baseline and the cap is shown for context only.

Sensitivity. Each pooled value is recomputed under three parametric assumptions: log-normal (headline), gamma (alternative), and nonparametric reconstruction. The spread across the three is reported as the sensitivity band: stable (≤25%), provisional (25–50%), or needs_review (>50%). Confidence intervals are computed via meta-analytic bootstrap that resamples whole sources (not individual pseudo-samples) to capture between-source uncertainty.

Methodology authorities. The pooling approach follows WHO/JECFA pooled total-diet study methodology, the EFSA Scientific Opinions on lead (2010, 2024) and cadmium (2009, 2024) for log-normal contaminant occurrence reconstruction, ATSDR moment-matching guidance for summary-statistic-only sources, and Codex CAC/GL 50-2004 weighted pooling. Per-source contribution logs document every admission decision and every basis conversion.

Tooling and reproducibility. The pooling engine is tools/evidence/build-hmtc-aggregate-distributions.mjs (1170 lines of deterministic JavaScript, 37 unit tests). The briefing generator is tools/standards/build-standards-briefing.mjs (this file). Both are versioned in git and re-runnable. Every numeric claim in this briefing can be regenerated by checking out the commit named in the Provenance Footer and running npm run evidence:pooling && npm run briefing -- --product=infant-formula-powder-non-soy.

Per-Analyte Sections

Pb

Published HMTc threshold: 20 ppb [powder] · 20 ppb (pre-rounding) Cross-basis: ≈ 3 ppb [prepared] (÷7 (1:7 reconstitution)) Rationale tag: regulatory-alignment Regulatory ceiling reference: 20 ppb (eu2023_pb_formula_powder_20) Pool composition: 15 sources contributing · 236 pseudo-samples in pool · 1 sample-level contributors Bootstrap 95% CI: [4.06, 82.86] ppb Sensitivity: log-normal 57.48 · gamma 57.87 · nonparametric 59.43 ppb · spread 3% (stable)

Defensibility. The published value matches the lowest applicable regulatory ceiling (eu2023_pb_formula_powder_20). This is the strongest defense vector: HMTc certifies at the binding government limit, regardless of where the pooled literature value falls. Any attack on the underlying pool is moot, the cap binds. The pooled P97 of 62.97 ppb sits above the cap, confirming the cap is the protective constraint.

Brand legal read. This threshold matches the binding government cap (eu2023_pb_formula_powder_20). A Daubert challenge on the value reduces to "is eu2023_pb_formula_powder_20 a legitimate regulatory anchor?", and the answer is yes, it is published, finalized, and enforced.

Retailer compliance read. A certified product tests at or below 20 ppb (native basis: [powder]) for this analyte. The certification mark on shelf indicates the brand has met every value in the Master Limit Table simultaneously. Lab COA comparisons should be done in the basis stated; the cross-basis equivalent is provided for reference.

Regulator read. HMTc threshold = eu2023_pb_formula_powder_20 cap. The pooled occurrence distribution from 15 sources confirms the cap is the protective constraint; the cap binds at a value below where the pooled P97 falls.

iAs

Published HMTc threshold: 10 ppb [powder] · 8.655 ppb (pre-rounding) Cross-basis: ≈ 2 ppb [prepared] (÷7 (1:7 reconstitution)) Rationale tag: literature-baseline Regulatory ceiling reference: 20 ppb (eu2023_ias_formula_powder_20) Pool composition: 7 sources contributing · 52 pseudo-samples in pool · 0 sample-level contributors Bootstrap 95% CI: [4.71, 21.98] ppb Sensitivity: log-normal 11.45 · gamma 8.54 · nonparametric 14.11 ppb · spread 40% (provisional)

Defensibility. Pooled P97 from 7 contributing sources across multiple jurisdictions. No applicable regulatory ceiling for this analyte-matrix combination below the pooled value (cap = 20 ppb). The pooled value derives from the moment-matching methodology cited above; the contribution log lists every source's admission decision and basis conversion. Sensitivity band is provisional, log-normal, gamma, and nonparametric variants disagree by more than 25%, signaling heterogeneity that should be resolved before final publication. Likely sources: basis mixing, regional outliers, or sparse pseudo-sample contributions.

Brand legal read. The value is pooled P97 from 7 peer-reviewed and agency sources across multiple jurisdictions. Daubert defense rests on (a) the methodology is published, reproducible, and follows international precedent (WHO, EFSA, JECFA, ATSDR, Codex); (b) the source pool is global, not cherry-picked; (c) every numeric claim traces to a source page.

MeHg

Published HMTc threshold: 1 ppb [powder] · 0.787 ppb (pre-rounding) Cross-basis: ≈ 0.5 ppb [prepared] (÷7 (1:7 reconstitution)) Rationale tag: feasibility-driven Regulatory ceiling reference: EFSA TWI 1.3 µg/kg-bw/week (exposure context, not concentration cap) Pool composition: 0 sources contributing · 0 pseudo-samples in pool · 0 sample-level contributors feasibility-anchored (analytical LOQ binds)

Four-anchor read-across (this row has no direct MeHg occurrence data):

  1. Empirical anchor. Total Hg pooled P97 for this row = 1.575 ppb (8 contributing sources). MeHg ≤ tHg by chemistry; for a non-fish matrix the methylmercury fraction is typically below 50%.
  2. Analytical anchor. ICP-MS with cold-vapor hydride generation for MeHg in dairy-matrix formula has a typical LOQ of 0.1 µg/kg. Path B floor = LOQ × 5 = 0.5 ppb.
  3. Regulatory anchor. EFSA TWI = 1.3 µg/kg b.w./week (efsa-methylmercury-twi); EPA IRIS RfD = 0.1 µg/kg b.w./day (epa-iris-methylmercury-rfd); JECFA PTWI = 1.6 µg/kg b.w./week (jecfa-methylmercury-ptwi). At 0.5 ppb in formula × typical infant intake of ~150 mL/kg-bw/day, the MeHg contribution from formula is ~0.075 µg/kg-bw/week, roughly 6% of the EFSA TWI.
  4. Inferential anchor. Dairy supply chain does not biomagnify mercury. Cattle do not consume aquatic biomass; pasture water and feed contribute primarily inorganic Hg. The matrix is not a methylmercury vehicle.

Each anchor is independently citable. The published threshold is the maximum of the analytical floor and the read-across upper bound, which gives the smallest defensible value that a competent lab can verify. Tagged feasibility-driven because the analytical LOQ is the binding constraint today; this threshold ratchets tighter when MeHg-specific occurrence data for this matrix lands in the corpus (scheduled for Tier 2 review after Phase 1 backlog clears).

Defensibility. No direct occurrence data for this analyte in this matrix; the value is anchored by the four-anchor read-across documented above. The empirical anchor (parent species pool), analytical anchor (Path B LOQ × 5), regulatory anchor (EFSA/EPA/JECFA exposure framework), and inferential anchor (matrix chemistry) are independently defensible. A plaintiff's "no MeHg data" attack does not land because each anchor stands on its own; rebutting one does not invalidate the threshold.

Brand legal read. The threshold is the analytical-method floor (Path B LOQ × 5). Daubert defense is "you cannot publish a tighter limit than the standard analytical method can verify, and we cannot defensibly certify against a limit no lab can confirm." The four-anchor read-across documents that the matrix is empirically clean for this analyte.

Retailer compliance read. A certified product tests at or below 1 ppb (native basis: [powder]) for this analyte. The certification mark on shelf indicates the brand has met every value in the Master Limit Table simultaneously. Lab COA comparisons should be done in the basis stated; the cross-basis equivalent is provided for reference.

Regulator read. No direct MeHg occurrence data for this matrix in the structured corpus. Threshold is derived via four-anchor read-across documented above: empirical (total Hg pool for this row, see tHg row); analytical (ICP-MS-HG LOQ × 5); regulatory (EFSA TWI 1.3 µg/kg-bw/week + EPA RfD 0.1 µg/kg-bw/day + JECFA PTWI 1.6 µg/kg-bw/week as exposure ceiling); inferential (non-fish matrix chemistry). Methodology follows WHO/JECFA read-across precedent for analytes where direct occurrence data is absent but the parent species, the regulatory exposure framework, and the matrix biology are all citable.

Adversarial Read, what a plaintiff's expert will attack, and the response

This section explicitly addresses the most likely attack vectors a plaintiff's expert, a competing standards body, or a journalist working an adversarial angle will use against this briefing. Each attack is named and answered.

Attack 1: "The pool is too small; the methodology cherry-picks." Response: The pool admits every source that contributes a usable statistic (sample-level data, percentile vector, mean, median, max, lb/ub mean, range). No source is excluded for being "summary-only." The contribution log per analyte lists every admission decision and every basis conversion. Per-source pool share is capped at 50% to prevent dominance by any single survey. The methodology is reproducible from the structured CSVs in data/evidence/ and the engine in tools/evidence/build-hmtc-aggregate-distributions.mjs.

Attack 2: "Why P97 instead of P90? You're loosening the standard." Response: P97 per analyte is calibrated to deliver approximately 90% row-pass for the clean subcategory across the 10-analyte joint. Setting each analyte's limit at P90 of its own marginal would deliver a joint pass rate of 35% under independence and 50-70% under realistic correlation, well short of the Governing Principles' 90/30 row-pass target. P97 is the per-analyte percentile that delivers the row-level outcome the program promises. ALARA and the §80th-percentile ratchet trigger drive post-publication tightening.

Attack 3: "Why is your dirty subcategory P45 instead of P10?" Response: P45 of the dirty subcategory's distribution, applied per-analyte, delivers approximately 30% joint row-pass for dirty-subcategory brands. This is the design intent stated in the Governing Principles v2.0: the dirty subcategory standard must give dirty-platform brands a realistic path to certification so the program drives reformulation rather than excluding the platform. A P10 dirty standard would deliver <1% joint row-pass and the program would have no commercial uptake on the dirty side.

Attack 4: "Your threshold matches the EU/FDA cap; HMTc adds no value over the regulator." Response: For Pb, the published threshold matches the binding regulatory cap because the pooled P97 sits above the cap. HMTc cannot defensibly publish a tighter standard than the literature supports under its row-pass calibration. The value HMTc adds is row-level certification, a brand must meet every value in the Master Limit Table simultaneously on a like-for-like basis, with documented contribution logs and sensitivity analyses. Single-analyte regulatory caps do not deliver row-level certification.

Attack 5: "Your sensitivity analysis shows the methodology is unstable for some analytes." Response: The sensitivity bands are explicitly reported per analyte. 3 analytes (iAs, Al, tHg) are flagged provisional, log-normal, gamma, and nonparametric variants disagree by more than 25%. This is transparent reporting, not a methodological flaw. Provisional values are published with the band stated and a commitment to resolve before final publication. The alternative, silently picking one parametric form, would be the actual methodological flaw.

Attack 6: "Methylmercury, you have no data, but you published a value anyway." Response: The four-anchor read-across is documented in the MeHg per-analyte section. The empirical anchor (parent species pool: tHg ≈ 0 ppb in this matrix), the analytical anchor (Path B LOQ × 5), the regulatory anchor (EFSA TWI as exposure context), and the inferential anchor (non-fish matrix chemistry) are independently citable. The published value is the analytical floor, the smallest concentration a competent lab can verify. Tagged feasibility-driven with scheduled Tier 2 review when MeHg-specific occurrence data lands. The "no data" framing misrepresents the four-anchor defense.

Attack 7: "Where does basis conversion happen and what authority do you cite?" Response: The 1:7 powder ↔ prepared-for-feeding conversion is the conservative reconstitution ratio (1 g powder + 6 g water = 7 g prepared) standard across infant-formula regulatory frameworks (FDA Closer-to-Zero, EU 2023/915). Within-class conversions (as_sold ↔ dry_as_sold) pass through with factor 1. Every basis conversion is logged per source in the contribution log; unknown-conversion cases are flagged and not silently coerced.

Attack 8: "Show me one source you excluded." Response: No source contributing a usable statistic is excluded. Sources marked "no_usable_statistic" in the contribution log are LOD-censored entries with neither mean nor lb/ub recorded. Sources without a structured CSV row are documented in the routing audit and the gap report; those are extraction gaps, not exclusions, and they are tracked in Phase 1 backlog with target completion timelines.

3.2 Infant Formula, Powder (Soy-Based)

This chapter is one of fifteen per-product chapters in Part 3 of the HMTc Program Manual. Each chapter is self-contained enough to function as a licensing exhibit detached from the bound volume. The methodology that produces the limit values in this chapter is documented in Part 5.1 (How HMTc Standards Are Set). The certification status framework that operationalizes the limits is documented in Part 2.3. The Standards Ratchet that evolves these limits over time is documented in Part 2.10. Program-level adversarial attacks not specific to this product are addressed in Part 5.2 (Program-Level Adversarial Read); the per-product adversarial read in this chapter addresses attacks specific to this product category.

Product class: Infant Formula, Powder (Soy-Based) Subcategory role: dirty subcategory (within-pair) Native basis for published thresholds: powder_as_placed Methodology: HMTc-10 analyte panel; per-analyte standards derived from pooled occurrence distribution at P97 (clean / independent rows) or P45 (dirty rows), capped at the lowest applicable regulatory ceiling. See "Methodology in one page" below. Date generated: 2026-05-14 Author: Karen Pendergrass, Standards Architect, Paleo Foundation Status: Draft for stakeholder review. Who this briefing is for

The HMTc certification mark serves multiple audiences with different concerns. This briefing is written so each audience finds what they need in one document. Audience-tagged "read" callouts appear inline next to each per-analyte section.

Brand legal teams, Read the per-analyte Defensibility narrative and the Adversarial read section. The methodology section is your Daubert-resistance reference. Every numeric claim traces to a source page; the contribution logs document admission criteria.

Retailer quality and compliance, Read the Master Limit Table and the per-analyte Regulator read callouts. The certification mark on a product means the brand has tested at or below every value in the Master Limit Table, in the product's native basis. The regulatory comparison rows show which government caps apply and how HMTc thresholds compare.

Regulators (FDA, EFSA, state AGs), Read the Methodology section, the Sources Cited table, and the Anti-Circumvention argument. The methodology is open, reproducible, and follows the WHO/JECFA, EFSA, ATSDR, and Codex precedents cited. Numeric thresholds are derived from a pooled occurrence distribution of peer-reviewed and agency data, weighted by source N with a 50% relative-share cap.

Adversarial readers (plaintiff's experts, competing standards bodies), Read the per-analyte Defensibility narrative, the Adversarial read section, the contribution logs, and the sensitivity analyses. Every value carries: the pool composition, the basis-conversion math, the regulatory cap comparison, the parametric sensitivity, and the rationale for any gap between literature and threshold.

How to read this table. "Published" values are rounded up to the HMTc primary increment ladder (1, 2, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25, 50, 100, 200, 500, 1000, 1300, 1500, 2000, 5000, 10000 ppb) per the program's publication conventions; this gives brands an unambiguous bin to test against. The "precise" column shows the pooled-engine output before rounding so the methodology is transparent and re-derivable. Rationale tags map to the four allowed rationale tags: literature-baseline (pooled value binds), regulatory-alignment (regulatory ceiling binds), feasibility-driven (analytical method binds), and precautionary (manual override below pooled and regulatory).

Methodology, one page

Per-analyte standards. For each (product row, analyte) cell, every source loaded for that cell contributes to a pooled distribution via moment-matching pseudo-sample expansion. Sample-level data uses raw values; summary statistics (mean, median, max, lb/ub, reported percentiles) reconstruct under log-normal or Tobit assumptions documented per source. Pseudo-samples are weighted by source N and capped at 50% relative pool share to prevent any one survey from dominating. The pooled distribution is sorted and queried at the per-analyte target percentile:

  • Clean subcategory: standard = pooled P97
  • Dirty subcategory: standard = pooled P45
  • Independent (no within-pair partner): standard = pooled P97 by default

Why P97 / P45, not P90 / P30 (the row-pass calibration). A brand certifies a product (one row, ten analytes simultaneously), not one analyte at a time. Setting each per-analyte limit at the raw P90 of the clean distribution would deliver a joint row-pass rate close to 0.9^10 = 35% under analyte independence. Under realistic analyte correlation (~0.6, since supply-chain hygiene drives multiple metals together), the joint rate sits around 50-70%. To deliver the row-level outcomes the Governing Principles require, approximately 90% of clean-subcategory brands and approximately 30% of dirty-subcategory brands pass row certification, per-analyte limits are uplifted to P97 (clean) and P45 (dirty). This is the calibration that delivers the program's stated objective. ALARA and the §80th-percentile ratchet trigger drive post-publication tightening; per-analyte differentiation (e.g., Pb tighter than Ni) is deferred until HMTc has 6-12 months of lot-testing data to measure actual joint pass rates.

Basis. Each product row's standard is published in the native basis of the product form: powder rows in powder-as-placed-on-market, ready-to-feed liquid rows in as-consumed, dry cereal rows in dry-as-sold, puree rows in as-consumed. Brands ship and test in their product's native form; the certification mark matches that form. Sources reporting in non-native bases are converted into the row's native basis during pooling via documented factors (the 1:7 reconstitution ratio for powder ↔ prepared; within-class identity for as_sold ↔ dry_as_sold). The cross-basis equivalent of every published value is shown in the Master Limit Table for ease of reference by audiences operating in different bases.

Regulatory cap. The final published HMTc value never exceeds the lowest applicable loaded regulatory ceiling, expressed in the product's native basis. When the pooled P97/P45 exceeds the regulatory cap, the cap binds and the rationale tag is regulatory-alignment. When the pooled value is below the cap, the rationale is literature-baseline and the cap is shown for context only.

Sensitivity. Each pooled value is recomputed under three parametric assumptions: log-normal (headline), gamma (alternative), and nonparametric reconstruction. The spread across the three is reported as the sensitivity band: stable (≤25%), provisional (25–50%), or needs_review (>50%). Confidence intervals are computed via meta-analytic bootstrap that resamples whole sources (not individual pseudo-samples) to capture between-source uncertainty.

Methodology authorities. The pooling approach follows WHO/JECFA pooled total-diet study methodology, the EFSA Scientific Opinions on lead (2010, 2024) and cadmium (2009, 2024) for log-normal contaminant occurrence reconstruction, ATSDR moment-matching guidance for summary-statistic-only sources, and Codex CAC/GL 50-2004 weighted pooling. Per-source contribution logs document every admission decision and every basis conversion.

Tooling and reproducibility. The pooling engine is tools/evidence/build-hmtc-aggregate-distributions.mjs (1170 lines of deterministic JavaScript, 37 unit tests). The briefing generator is tools/standards/build-standards-briefing.mjs (this file). Both are versioned in git and re-runnable. Every numeric claim in this briefing can be regenerated by checking out the commit named in the Provenance Footer and running npm run evidence:pooling && npm run briefing -- --product=infant-formula-powder-soy-based.

Adversarial Read, what a plaintiff's expert will attack, and the response

This section explicitly addresses the most likely attack vectors a plaintiff's expert, a competing standards body, or a journalist working an adversarial angle will use against this briefing. Each attack is named and answered.

Attack 1: "The pool is too small; the methodology cherry-picks." Response: The pool admits every source that contributes a usable statistic (sample-level data, percentile vector, mean, median, max, lb/ub mean, range). No source is excluded for being "summary-only." The contribution log per analyte lists every admission decision and every basis conversion. Per-source pool share is capped at 50% to prevent dominance by any single survey. The methodology is reproducible from the structured CSVs in data/evidence/ and the engine in tools/evidence/build-hmtc-aggregate-distributions.mjs.

Attack 2: "Why P97 instead of P90? You're loosening the standard." Response: P97 per analyte is calibrated to deliver approximately 90% row-pass for the clean subcategory across the 10-analyte joint. Setting each analyte's limit at P90 of its own marginal would deliver a joint pass rate of 35% under independence and 50-70% under realistic correlation, well short of the Governing Principles' 90/30 row-pass target. P97 is the per-analyte percentile that delivers the row-level outcome the program promises. ALARA and the §80th-percentile ratchet trigger drive post-publication tightening.

Attack 3: "Why is your dirty subcategory P45 instead of P10?" Response: P45 of the dirty subcategory's distribution, applied per-analyte, delivers approximately 30% joint row-pass for dirty-subcategory brands. This is the design intent stated in the Governing Principles v2.0: the dirty subcategory standard must give dirty-platform brands a realistic path to certification so the program drives reformulation rather than excluding the platform. A P10 dirty standard would deliver <1% joint row-pass and the program would have no commercial uptake on the dirty side.

Attack 5: "Your sensitivity analysis shows the methodology is unstable for some analytes." Response: The sensitivity bands are explicitly reported per analyte. 1 analytes (Al) are flagged provisional, log-normal, gamma, and nonparametric variants disagree by more than 25%. This is transparent reporting, not a methodological flaw. Provisional values are published with the band stated and a commitment to resolve before final publication. The alternative, silently picking one parametric form, would be the actual methodological flaw.

Attack 7: "Where does basis conversion happen and what authority do you cite?" Response: The 1:7 powder ↔ prepared-for-feeding conversion is the conservative reconstitution ratio (1 g powder + 6 g water = 7 g prepared) standard across infant-formula regulatory frameworks (FDA Closer-to-Zero, EU 2023/915). Within-class conversions (as_sold ↔ dry_as_sold) pass through with factor 1. Every basis conversion is logged per source in the contribution log; unknown-conversion cases are flagged and not silently coerced.

Attack 8: "Show me one source you excluded." Response: No source contributing a usable statistic is excluded. Sources marked "no_usable_statistic" in the contribution log are LOD-censored entries with neither mean nor lb/ub recorded. Sources without a structured CSV row are documented in the routing audit and the gap report; those are extraction gaps, not exclusions, and they are tracked in Phase 1 backlog with target completion timelines.

3.3 Infant Formula, Ready-to-Feed Liquid (Non-Soy)

This chapter is one of fifteen per-product chapters in Part 3 of the HMTc Program Manual. Each chapter is self-contained enough to function as a licensing exhibit detached from the bound volume. The methodology that produces the limit values in this chapter is documented in Part 5.1 (How HMTc Standards Are Set). The certification status framework that operationalizes the limits is documented in Part 2.3. The Standards Ratchet that evolves these limits over time is documented in Part 2.10. Program-level adversarial attacks not specific to this product are addressed in Part 5.2 (Program-Level Adversarial Read); the per-product adversarial read in this chapter addresses attacks specific to this product category.

Product class: Infant Formula, RTF Liquid (Non-Soy) Subcategory role: clean subcategory (within-pair) Native basis for published thresholds: as_consumed Methodology: HMTc-10 analyte panel; per-analyte standards derived from pooled occurrence distribution at P97 (clean / independent rows) or P45 (dirty rows), capped at the lowest applicable regulatory ceiling. See "Methodology in one page" below. Date generated: 2026-05-14 Author: Karen Pendergrass, Standards Architect, Paleo Foundation Status: Draft for stakeholder review. Who this briefing is for

The HMTc certification mark serves multiple audiences with different concerns. This briefing is written so each audience finds what they need in one document. Audience-tagged "read" callouts appear inline next to each per-analyte section.

Brand legal teams, Read the per-analyte Defensibility narrative and the Adversarial read section. The methodology section is your Daubert-resistance reference. Every numeric claim traces to a source page; the contribution logs document admission criteria.

Retailer quality and compliance, Read the Master Limit Table and the per-analyte Regulator read callouts. The certification mark on a product means the brand has tested at or below every value in the Master Limit Table, in the product's native basis. The regulatory comparison rows show which government caps apply and how HMTc thresholds compare.

Regulators (FDA, EFSA, state AGs), Read the Methodology section, the Sources Cited table, and the Anti-Circumvention argument. The methodology is open, reproducible, and follows the WHO/JECFA, EFSA, ATSDR, and Codex precedents cited. Numeric thresholds are derived from a pooled occurrence distribution of peer-reviewed and agency data, weighted by source N with a 50% relative-share cap.

Adversarial readers (plaintiff's experts, competing standards bodies), Read the per-analyte Defensibility narrative, the Adversarial read section, the contribution logs, and the sensitivity analyses. Every value carries: the pool composition, the basis-conversion math, the regulatory cap comparison, the parametric sensitivity, and the rationale for any gap between literature and threshold.

All ten HMTc analytes for this product class. How to read this table. "Published" values are rounded up to the HMTc primary increment ladder (1, 2, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25, 50, 100, 200, 500, 1000, 1300, 1500, 2000, 5000, 10000 ppb) per the program's publication conventions; this gives brands an unambiguous bin to test against. The "precise" column shows the pooled-engine output before rounding so the methodology is transparent and re-derivable. Rationale tags map to the four allowed rationale tags: literature-baseline (pooled value binds), regulatory-alignment (regulatory ceiling binds), feasibility-driven (analytical method binds), and precautionary (manual override below pooled and regulatory).

Methodology, one page

Per-analyte standards. For each (product row, analyte) cell, every source loaded for that cell contributes to a pooled distribution via moment-matching pseudo-sample expansion. Sample-level data uses raw values; summary statistics (mean, median, max, lb/ub, reported percentiles) reconstruct under log-normal or Tobit assumptions documented per source. Pseudo-samples are weighted by source N and capped at 50% relative pool share to prevent any one survey from dominating. The pooled distribution is sorted and queried at the per-analyte target percentile:

  • Clean subcategory: standard = pooled P97
  • Dirty subcategory: standard = pooled P45
  • Independent (no within-pair partner): standard = pooled P97 by default

Why P97 / P45, not P90 / P30 (the row-pass calibration). A brand certifies a product (one row, ten analytes simultaneously), not one analyte at a time. Setting each per-analyte limit at the raw P90 of the clean distribution would deliver a joint row-pass rate close to 0.9^10 = 35% under analyte independence. Under realistic analyte correlation (~0.6, since supply-chain hygiene drives multiple metals together), the joint rate sits around 50-70%. To deliver the row-level outcomes the Governing Principles require, approximately 90% of clean-subcategory brands and approximately 30% of dirty-subcategory brands pass row certification, per-analyte limits are uplifted to P97 (clean) and P45 (dirty). This is the calibration that delivers the program's stated objective. ALARA and the §80th-percentile ratchet trigger drive post-publication tightening; per-analyte differentiation (e.g., Pb tighter than Ni) is deferred until HMTc has 6-12 months of lot-testing data to measure actual joint pass rates.

Basis. Each product row's standard is published in the native basis of the product form: powder rows in powder-as-placed-on-market, ready-to-feed liquid rows in as-consumed, dry cereal rows in dry-as-sold, puree rows in as-consumed. Brands ship and test in their product's native form; the certification mark matches that form. Sources reporting in non-native bases are converted into the row's native basis during pooling via documented factors (the 1:7 reconstitution ratio for powder ↔ prepared; within-class identity for as_sold ↔ dry_as_sold). The cross-basis equivalent of every published value is shown in the Master Limit Table for ease of reference by audiences operating in different bases.

Regulatory cap. The final published HMTc value never exceeds the lowest applicable loaded regulatory ceiling, expressed in the product's native basis. When the pooled P97/P45 exceeds the regulatory cap, the cap binds and the rationale tag is regulatory-alignment. When the pooled value is below the cap, the rationale is literature-baseline and the cap is shown for context only.

Sensitivity. Each pooled value is recomputed under three parametric assumptions: log-normal (headline), gamma (alternative), and nonparametric reconstruction. The spread across the three is reported as the sensitivity band: stable (≤25%), provisional (25–50%), or needs_review (>50%). Confidence intervals are computed via meta-analytic bootstrap that resamples whole sources (not individual pseudo-samples) to capture between-source uncertainty.

Methodology authorities. The pooling approach follows WHO/JECFA pooled total-diet study methodology, the EFSA Scientific Opinions on lead (2010, 2024) and cadmium (2009, 2024) for log-normal contaminant occurrence reconstruction, ATSDR moment-matching guidance for summary-statistic-only sources, and Codex CAC/GL 50-2004 weighted pooling. Per-source contribution logs document every admission decision and every basis conversion.

Tooling and reproducibility. The pooling engine is tools/evidence/build-hmtc-aggregate-distributions.mjs (1170 lines of deterministic JavaScript, 37 unit tests). The briefing generator is tools/standards/build-standards-briefing.mjs (this file). Both are versioned in git and re-runnable. Every numeric claim in this briefing can be regenerated by checking out the commit named in the Provenance Footer and running npm run evidence:pooling && npm run briefing -- --product=infant-formula-rtf-liquid-non-soy.

Adversarial Read, what a plaintiff's expert will attack, and the response

This section explicitly addresses the most likely attack vectors a plaintiff's expert, a competing standards body, or a journalist working an adversarial angle will use against this briefing. Each attack is named and answered.

Attack 1: "The pool is too small; the methodology cherry-picks." Response: The pool admits every source that contributes a usable statistic (sample-level data, percentile vector, mean, median, max, lb/ub mean, range). No source is excluded for being "summary-only." The contribution log per analyte lists every admission decision and every basis conversion. Per-source pool share is capped at 50% to prevent dominance by any single survey. The methodology is reproducible from the structured CSVs in data/evidence/ and the engine in tools/evidence/build-hmtc-aggregate-distributions.mjs.

Attack 2: "Why P97 instead of P90? You're loosening the standard." Response: P97 per analyte is calibrated to deliver approximately 90% row-pass for the clean subcategory across the 10-analyte joint. Setting each analyte's limit at P90 of its own marginal would deliver a joint pass rate of 35% under independence and 50-70% under realistic correlation, well short of the Governing Principles' 90/30 row-pass target. P97 is the per-analyte percentile that delivers the row-level outcome the program promises. ALARA and the §80th-percentile ratchet trigger drive post-publication tightening.

Attack 3: "Why is your dirty subcategory P45 instead of P10?" Response: P45 of the dirty subcategory's distribution, applied per-analyte, delivers approximately 30% joint row-pass for dirty-subcategory brands. This is the design intent stated in the Governing Principles v2.0: the dirty subcategory standard must give dirty-platform brands a realistic path to certification so the program drives reformulation rather than excluding the platform. A P10 dirty standard would deliver <1% joint row-pass and the program would have no commercial uptake on the dirty side.

Attack 7: "Where does basis conversion happen and what authority do you cite?" Response: The 1:7 powder ↔ prepared-for-feeding conversion is the conservative reconstitution ratio (1 g powder + 6 g water = 7 g prepared) standard across infant-formula regulatory frameworks (FDA Closer-to-Zero, EU 2023/915). Within-class conversions (as_sold ↔ dry_as_sold) pass through with factor 1. Every basis conversion is logged per source in the contribution log; unknown-conversion cases are flagged and not silently coerced.

Attack 8: "Show me one source you excluded." Response: No source contributing a usable statistic is excluded. Sources marked "no_usable_statistic" in the contribution log are LOD-censored entries with neither mean nor lb/ub recorded. Sources without a structured CSV row are documented in the routing audit and the gap report; those are extraction gaps, not exclusions, and they are tracked in Phase 1 backlog with target completion timelines.

To deliver the row-level outcomes the Governing Principles require, approximately 90% of clean-subcategory brands and approximately 30% of dirty-subcategory brands pass row certification, per-analyte limits are uplifted to P97 (clean) and P45 (dirty). This is the calibration that delivers the program's stated objective. ALARA and the §80th-percentile ratchet trigger drive post-publication tightening; per-analyte differentiation (e.g., Pb tighter than Ni) is deferred until HMTc has 6-12 months of lot-testing data to measure actual joint pass rates.

Basis. Each product row's standard is published in the native basis of the product form: powder rows in powder-as-placed-on-market, ready-to-feed liquid rows in as-consumed, dry cereal rows in dry-as-sold, puree rows in as-consumed. Brands ship and test in their product's native form; the certification mark matches that form. Sources reporting in non-native bases are converted into the row's native basis during pooling via documented factors (the 1:7 reconstitution ratio for powder ↔ prepared; within-class identity for as_sold ↔ dry_as_sold). The cross-basis equivalent of every published value is shown in the Master Limit Table for ease of reference by audiences operating in different bases.

Regulatory cap. The final published HMTc value never exceeds the lowest applicable loaded regulatory ceiling, expressed in the product's native basis. When the pooled P97/P45 exceeds the regulatory cap, the cap binds and the rationale tag is regulatory-alignment. When the pooled value is below the cap, the rationale is literature-baseline and the cap is shown for context only.

Sensitivity. Each pooled value is recomputed under three parametric assumptions: log-normal (headline), gamma (alternative), and nonparametric reconstruction. The spread across the three is reported as the sensitivity band: stable (≤25%), provisional (25–50%), or needs_review (>50%). Confidence intervals are computed via meta-analytic bootstrap that resamples whole sources (not individual pseudo-samples) to capture between-source uncertainty.

Methodology authorities. The pooling approach follows WHO/JECFA pooled total-diet study methodology, the EFSA Scientific Opinions on lead (2010, 2024) and cadmium (2009, 2024) for log-normal contaminant occurrence reconstruction, ATSDR moment-matching guidance for summary-statistic-only sources, and Codex CAC/GL 50-2004 weighted pooling. Per-source contribution logs document every admission decision and every basis conversion.

Tooling and reproducibility. The pooling engine is tools/evidence/build-hmtc-aggregate-distributions.mjs (1170 lines of deterministic JavaScript, 37 unit tests). The briefing generator is tools/standards/build-standards-briefing.mjs (this file). Both are versioned in git and re-runnable. Every numeric claim in this briefing can be regenerated by checking out the commit named in the Provenance Footer and running npm run evidence:pooling && npm run briefing -- --product=infant-formula-rtf-liquid-soy-based.

Adversarial Read, what a plaintiff's expert will attack, and the response

This section explicitly addresses the most likely attack vectors a plaintiff's expert, a competing standards body, or a journalist working an adversarial angle will use against this briefing. Each attack is named and answered.

Attack 1: "The pool is too small; the methodology cherry-picks." Response: The pool admits every source that contributes a usable statistic (sample-level data, percentile vector, mean, median, max, lb/ub mean, range). No source is excluded for being "summary-only." The contribution log per analyte lists every admission decision and every basis conversion. Per-source pool share is capped at 50% to prevent dominance by any single survey. The methodology is reproducible from the structured CSVs in data/evidence/ and the engine in tools/evidence/build-hmtc-aggregate-distributions.mjs.

Attack 2: "Why P97 instead of P90? You're loosening the standard." Response: P97 per analyte is calibrated to deliver approximately 90% row-pass for the clean subcategory across the 10-analyte joint. Setting each analyte's limit at P90 of its own marginal would deliver a joint pass rate of 35% under independence and 50-70% under realistic correlation, well short of the Governing Principles' 90/30 row-pass target. P97 is the per-analyte percentile that delivers the row-level outcome the program promises. ALARA and the §80th-percentile ratchet trigger drive post-publication tightening.

Attack 3: "Why is your dirty subcategory P45 instead of P10?" Response: P45 of the dirty subcategory's distribution, applied per-analyte, delivers approximately 30% joint row-pass for dirty-subcategory brands. This is the design intent stated in the Governing Principles v2.0: the dirty subcategory standard must give dirty-platform brands a realistic path to certification so the program drives reformulation rather than excluding the platform. A P10 dirty standard would deliver <1% joint row-pass and the program would have no commercial uptake on the dirty side.

Attack 7: "Where does basis conversion happen and what authority do you cite?" Response: The 1:7 powder ↔ prepared-for-feeding conversion is the conservative reconstitution ratio (1 g powder + 6 g water = 7 g prepared) standard across infant-formula regulatory frameworks (FDA Closer-to-Zero, EU 2023/915). Within-class conversions (as_sold ↔ dry_as_sold) pass through with factor 1. Every basis conversion is logged per source in the contribution log; unknown-conversion cases are flagged and not silently coerced.

Attack 8: "Show me one source you excluded." Response: No source contributing a usable statistic is excluded. Sources marked "no_usable_statistic" in the contribution log are LOD-censored entries with neither mean nor lb/ub recorded. Sources without a structured CSV row are documented in the routing audit and the gap report; those are extraction gaps, not exclusions, and they are tracked in Phase 1 backlog with target completion timelines.

This chapter is one of fifteen per-product chapters in Part 3 of the HMTc Program Manual. Each chapter is self-contained enough to function as a licensing exhibit detached from the bound volume. Who this briefing is for

The HMTc certification mark serves multiple audiences with different concerns. This briefing is written so each audience finds what they need in one document. Audience-tagged "read" callouts appear inline next to each per-analyte section.

Brand legal teams, Read the per-analyte Defensibility narrative and the Adversarial read section. The methodology section is your Daubert-resistance reference. Every numeric claim traces to a source page; the contribution logs document admission criteria.

Retailer quality and compliance, Read the Master Limit Table and the per-analyte Regulator read callouts. The certification mark on a product means the brand has tested at or below every value in the Master Limit Table, in the product's native basis. The regulatory comparison rows show which government caps apply and how HMTc thresholds compare.

Regulators (FDA, EFSA, state AGs), Read the Methodology section, the Sources Cited table, and the Anti-Circumvention argument. The methodology is open, reproducible, and follows the WHO/JECFA, EFSA, ATSDR, and Codex precedents cited. Numeric thresholds are derived from a pooled occurrence distribution of peer-reviewed and agency data, weighted by source N with a 50% relative-share cap.

Adversarial readers (plaintiff's experts, competing standards bodies), Read the per-analyte Defensibility narrative, the Adversarial read section, the contribution logs, and the sensitivity analyses. Every value carries: the pool composition, the basis-conversion math, the regulatory cap comparison, the parametric sensitivity, and the rationale for any gap between literature and threshold.

How to read this table. "Published" values are rounded up to the HMTc primary increment ladder (1, 2, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25, 50, 100, 200, 500, 1000, 1300, 1500, 2000, 5000, 10000 ppb) per the program's publication conventions; this gives brands an unambiguous bin to test against. The "precise" column shows the pooled-engine output before rounding so the methodology is transparent and re-derivable. Rationale tags map to the four allowed rationale tags: literature-baseline (pooled value binds), regulatory-alignment (regulatory ceiling binds), feasibility-driven (analytical method binds), and precautionary (manual override below pooled and regulatory).

Methodology, one page

Per-analyte standards. For each (product row, analyte) cell, every source loaded for that cell contributes to a pooled distribution via moment-matching pseudo-sample expansion. Sample-level data uses raw values; summary statistics (mean, median, max, lb/ub, reported percentiles) reconstruct under log-normal or Tobit assumptions documented per source. Pseudo-samples are weighted by source N and capped at 50% relative pool share to prevent any one survey from dominating. The pooled distribution is sorted and queried at the per-analyte target percentile:

  • Clean subcategory: standard = pooled P97
  • Dirty subcategory: standard = pooled P45
  • Independent (no within-pair partner): standard = pooled P97 by default

Why P97 / P45, not P90 / P30 (the row-pass calibration). A brand certifies a product (one row, ten analytes simultaneously), not one analyte at a time. Setting each per-analyte limit at the raw P90 of the clean distribution would deliver a joint row-pass rate close to 0.9^10 = 35% under analyte independence. Under realistic analyte correlation (~0.6, since supply-chain hygiene drives multiple metals together), the joint rate sits around 50-70%. To deliver the row-level outcomes the Governing Principles require, approximately 90% of clean-subcategory brands and approximately 30% of dirty-subcategory brands pass row certification, per-analyte limits are uplifted to P97 (clean) and P45 (dirty). This is the calibration that delivers the program's stated objective. ALARA and the §80th-percentile ratchet trigger drive post-publication tightening; per-analyte differentiation (e.g., Pb tighter than Ni) is deferred until HMTc has 6-12 months of lot-testing data to measure actual joint pass rates.

Basis. Each product row's standard is published in the native basis of the product form: powder rows in powder-as-placed-on-market, ready-to-feed liquid rows in as-consumed, dry cereal rows in dry-as-sold, puree rows in as-consumed. Brands ship and test in their product's native form; the certification mark matches that form. Sources reporting in non-native bases are converted into the row's native basis during pooling via documented factors (the 1:7 reconstitution ratio for powder ↔ prepared; within-class identity for as_sold ↔ dry_as_sold). The cross-basis equivalent of every published value is shown in the Master Limit Table for ease of reference by audiences operating in different bases.

Regulatory cap. The final published HMTc value never exceeds the lowest applicable loaded regulatory ceiling, expressed in the product's native basis. When the pooled P97/P45 exceeds the regulatory cap, the cap binds and the rationale tag is regulatory-alignment. When the pooled value is below the cap, the rationale is literature-baseline and the cap is shown for context only.

Sensitivity. Each pooled value is recomputed under three parametric assumptions: log-normal (headline), gamma (alternative), and nonparametric reconstruction. The spread across the three is reported as the sensitivity band: stable (≤25%), provisional (25–50%), or needs_review (>50%). Confidence intervals are computed via meta-analytic bootstrap that resamples whole sources (not individual pseudo-samples) to capture between-source uncertainty.

Methodology authorities. The pooling approach follows WHO/JECFA pooled total-diet study methodology, the EFSA Scientific Opinions on lead (2010, 2024) and cadmium (2009, 2024) for log-normal contaminant occurrence reconstruction, ATSDR moment-matching guidance for summary-statistic-only sources, and Codex CAC/GL 50-2004 weighted pooling. Per-source contribution logs document every admission decision and every basis conversion.

Tooling and reproducibility. The pooling engine is tools/evidence/build-hmtc-aggregate-distributions.mjs (1170 lines of deterministic JavaScript, 37 unit tests). The briefing generator is tools/standards/build-standards-briefing.mjs (this file). Both are versioned in git and re-runnable. Every numeric claim in this briefing can be regenerated by checking out the commit named in the Provenance Footer and running npm run evidence:pooling && npm run briefing -- --product=baby-cereals-dry-rice-based.

Brand legal read. The value is pooled P97 from 4 peer-reviewed and agency sources across multiple jurisdictions. Daubert defense rests on (a) the methodology is published, reproducible, and follows international precedent (WHO, EFSA, JECFA, ATSDR, Codex); (b) the source pool is global, not cherry-picked; (c) every numeric claim traces to a source page.

Retailer compliance read. A certified product tests at or below 20 ppb (native basis: [dry]) for this analyte. The certification mark on shelf indicates the brand has met every value in the Master Limit Table simultaneously. Lab COA comparisons should be done in the basis stated; the cross-basis equivalent is provided for reference.

Regulator read. Pooled methodology follows WHO/JECFA and EFSA precedent for total-diet-study pooling. 4 contributing sources across multiple jurisdictions. Sensitivity band stable.

Adversarial Read, what a plaintiff's expert will attack, and the response

This section explicitly addresses the most likely attack vectors a plaintiff's expert, a competing standards body, or a journalist working an adversarial angle will use against this briefing. Each attack is named and answered.

Attack 1: "The pool is too small; the methodology cherry-picks." Response: The pool admits every source that contributes a usable statistic (sample-level data, percentile vector, mean, median, max, lb/ub mean, range). No source is excluded for being "summary-only." The contribution log per analyte lists every admission decision and every basis conversion. Per-source pool share is capped at 50% to prevent dominance by any single survey. The methodology is reproducible from the structured CSVs in data/evidence/ and the engine in tools/evidence/build-hmtc-aggregate-distributions.mjs.

Attack 2: "Why P97 instead of P90? You're loosening the standard." Response: P97 per analyte is calibrated to deliver approximately 90% row-pass for the clean subcategory across the 10-analyte joint. Setting each analyte's limit at P90 of its own marginal would deliver a joint pass rate of 35% under independence and 50-70% under realistic correlation, well short of the Governing Principles' 90/30 row-pass target. P97 is the per-analyte percentile that delivers the row-level outcome the program promises. ALARA and the §80th-percentile ratchet trigger drive post-publication tightening.

Attack 3: "Why is your dirty subcategory P45 instead of P10?" Response: P45 of the dirty subcategory's distribution, applied per-analyte, delivers approximately 30% joint row-pass for dirty-subcategory brands. This is the design intent stated in the Governing Principles v2.0: the dirty subcategory standard must give dirty-platform brands a realistic path to certification so the program drives reformulation rather than excluding the platform. A P10 dirty standard would deliver <1% joint row-pass and the program would have no commercial uptake on the dirty side.

Attack 7: "Where does basis conversion happen and what authority do you cite?" Response: The 1:7 powder ↔ prepared-for-feeding conversion is the conservative reconstitution ratio (1 g powder + 6 g water = 7 g prepared) standard across infant-formula regulatory frameworks (FDA Closer-to-Zero, EU 2023/915). Within-class conversions (as_sold ↔ dry_as_sold) pass through with factor 1. Every basis conversion is logged per source in the contribution log; unknown-conversion cases are flagged and not silently coerced.

Attack 8: "Show me one source you excluded." Response: No source contributing a usable statistic is excluded. Sources marked "no_usable_statistic" in the contribution log are LOD-censored entries with neither mean nor lb/ub recorded. Sources without a structured CSV row are documented in the routing audit and the gap report; those are extraction gaps, not exclusions, and they are tracked in Phase 1 backlog with target completion timelines.
